Let me look at the card.md too, and check the Rocq version.

This is a noninterference proof for SLam calculus with if-then-else. Need to construct a logical relation.

Let me think about the structure. This is essentially the "security noninterference" benchmark from the "slang" / "plin" family. Known task: Tse? Actually this resembles the "Security Noninterference" benchmark from the POPLmark? It's from the "Logical Relations" tutorial by Amin & Rompf? Actually the style resembles the "slam" calculus in the "Noninterference" exercise from the "Software Foundations" volume? Hmm.

Let me think from scratch.

We need:
- definition of two terms being "equivalent" at a security level / type.
- Logical relation indexed by a "level" (label?) representing the observer's level.

Standard approach for SLam (security lambda calculus with types annotated by security levels):
Define relation `R : label -> ty -> tm -> tm -> Prop` meaning t1 and t2 are indistinguishable at level l (i.e., to an observer at level l). But here there are two labels: reader and indirect_reader. The security of a type kappa has reader and indirect_reader. Types are annotated with security records.

Common approach (from the paper "A Semantic Model for Noninterference"? Actually the "SLam" calculus from "Security Types Preserving Compilation" by Barthe et al?) Hmm.

Let me think about what the theorem says: two closed terms t1, t2 of same secret type T_secret placed in the same context C typed at T_result. If T_result is ground, transparent, and indirect_reader(T_secret) does not flow to indirect_reader(T_result), then the results are equal after erasure.

This is a termination-insensitive noninterference statement. We need a logical relation that captures "equivalence at a given observer level" of values and terms.

A standard technique: define a relation `equiv (l : label) (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm)` where l is the observer's indirect_reader level? Actually since only closed terms and closed contexts, the relation should tie: two terms are related at type T with respect to level l if they are both of type T and any pair of evaluation results v1, v2 are `erased-equal` if l flows... Hmm.

Actually since same_result is defined by erase_security equality of results, the "observational equivalence" at level ... The theorem requires equality of erased values, not just at some level. And `erase_security` erases all annotations, so it's structural equality of fully erased terms. Hmm, but the theorem is: if indirect_reader(T_secret) does not flow to indirect_reader(T_result), then plug C t1 and plug C t2 give equal results after erasure. This is a standard "secret inputs, public output" noninterference.

A common proof strategy for such benchmarks (from the "Noninterference" benchmark of the "CoqPL"? Actually this is benchmark 100 in the "Proof Discovery" benchmark? Let me check card.md).

Let me read card.md.

The key idea: to show noninterference at output type T_result (ground & transparent) when secret's indirect_reader does not flow to result's indirect_reader, one generally proves a "logical relation" indexed by a label (the observer level) that relates two terms at security level `l`, and prove:
1. Fundamental theorem: if `has_type Gamma t T`, and environments related, then terms related.
2. Related terms at level `l` evaluate to related values.

That's too strong; then the theorem would be trivial but unprovable for functions.

Better: define the logical relation as a *binary relation on terms* parameterized by a security level `l`, with value relation `V_l T v1 v2` and term relation `E_l T t1 t2 := forall v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 -> t2 -->* v2 -> value v1 -> value v2 -> V_l T v1 v2`.

This is the standard SLam/security STLC noninterference. I recall a similar benchmark: "Security noninterference for STLC" from the "Coq bench" with definitions `security`, `reader`, `indirect_reader`, `protect`, `ty_protect`, and a logical relation indexed by a label.

The classic SLam logical relation: define `V_ℓ(τ)` as:
- If τ = Ty_Unit κ: all values? Or require κ ... Hmm. In SLam, `τ` has a security annotation κ; the relation at level ℓ: terms of type τ are related if either κ is "above" ℓ (i.e., ℓ can't read it, so no constraint) or they're equal at level ℓ. Let me instead derive the needed relation from scratch.

Goal: same_result (plug C t1) (plug C t2): for any values v1, v2 with plug C ti -->* vi, erase_security v1 = erase_security v2.

Approach: define a relation `E (l : label) (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm)` intended: "t1 and t2 are indistinguishable to an observer at level l, assuming they have type T" — more precisely, the observer sees erased values, and if l can't read type T (i.e., ¬ flows_to l (security_of T).reader? or something), then no constraint; otherwise erased-equal structure.

Wait, but our conclusion is full erase equality, not relative to an observer. Hmm. But note that erase_security erases everything anyway, so erase equality is just syntactic equality up to annotations. Hmm, but the values will be equal because the terms are typed at T_result with ir Low: i.e., T_result's ir is Low means the result may be public; the context can't leak the secret into it. The level of the observer is T_result.ir; however erase equality is stronger than "low-observer equality" only in that the latter might ignore parts with High annotations.

Let me think about what values can arise at a ground type with security annotations. E.g., a value of type Ty_Pair (Ty_Unit High) (Ty_Unit High) κ. The value is tm_pair v1 v2 κ', where v1, v2 are units with high annotations. Since all units erase to tm_unit public, any two values of this type erase-equal if their structure matches (both pairs). So component annotations don't matter for erased equality: erased unit is always unit. So erased equality for ground values only depends on the *shape* (constructor tree), not annotations. Good.

For sums: erased equality requires same constructor at every sum node. So we need a relation on values that implies same shape.

The standard relation for noninterference with type annotations: at level l, a type T is "visible" if l can read it. If T's security has reader ≥ l? Hmm: information from T can flow to l if flows_to T.reader l (T's reader level flows to l). But also indirect_reader matters? For values, indirect_reader is about control flow; for the value itself, the reader annotation says who may read it, and indirect_reader says... in the security lattice, κ = (reader, ir) with wf κ: ir ≤ reader. A type Ty_Unit κ: an observer at level l can learn the value only if l ≥ κ.reader? Since reader denotes the level of the data itself. For an observer at level l, T is "opaque" if ¬ flows_to κ.reader l, in which case any two values are related (observer can't see). Otherwise must be equal (in the appropriate sense).

But our final goal is full erased equality; we'll need to get that from T_result's properties. Actually, let's look at the structure: `same_result` equality after erasure. The key theorem to prove about the logical relation likely is:

Lemma: If T is ground and transparent, and V_l T v1 v2 for l = security_of T's indirect_reader (or reader?), then erase_security v1 = erase_security v2.

Hmm. Let's think about what makes the relation "erased equal". Define the value relation V l T v1 v2 by induction:

Level parameter: Let's call it `l : label` (observer's indirect-reader level). Define:

Fixpoint V (l : label) (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop :=

  | Ty_Unit _ => True

```

```
But `flows_to ... l` is a Prop, not decidable; can't `if`. Use `flows_to κ.ir l -> structural` plus maybe leave universal when not. Better define:

V l T v1 v2 := match T with
 | Ty_Unit κ => True
 | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ => (flows_to κ.ir l -> (∃ v1' κ1 v2' κ2, v1 = tm_inl v1' κ1 ∧ v2 = tm_inl v2' κ2 ∧ V l T1 v1' v2') ∨ (∃ ..., inr...)) ∧ ??? 
Hmm, we also need structure even when opaque to make the monotonicity/typing lemmas work? If opaque, universal, fine. Let's define with an "if then ... else True" via implication: 

V l T v1 v2 := match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ =>
      flows_to κ.ir l ->
        (∃ v1' k1 v2' k2, v1 = tm_inl v1' k1 ∧ v2 = tm_inl v2' k2 ∧ V l T1 v1' v2')
        ∨ (∃ v1' k1 v2' k2, v1 = tm_inr v1' k1 ∧ v2 = tm_inr v2' k2 ∧ V l T2 v1' v2')
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ =>
      flows_to κ.ir l -> (∃ v1a v1b k1 v2a v2b k2, v1 = tm_pair v1a v1b k1 ∧ v2 = tm_pair v2a v2b k2 ∧ V l T1 v1a v2a ∧ V l T2 v1b v2b)
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ =>
      flows_to κ.ir l -> (forall u1 u2 r, V l T1 u1 u2 -> flows_to κ.reader r -> E l (ty_protect κ.ir T2) (tm_app v1 u1 r) (tm_app v2 u2 r))
  end

with E l T t1 t2 := forall v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 -> t2 -->* v2 -> value v1 -> value v2 -> V l T v1 v2.
```

Let me think about the intended semantics of the annotations:

- κ.reader: the level of the *value* (direct readers).
- κ.indirect_reader: the level of the *indirect* information, i.e., the fact that this term was evaluated / which computation led here (implicit flows).
- wf: indirect_reader ≤ reader (the indirect flow level is at most the direct level? Actually information about control is less secret than the data? Typing rules: elimination requires reader ⊑ r (the consumed value may flow to the elimination's result level r), and result is protected by ir: the fact that the elimination happened (control flow) is at level ir, so the result must be treated as having at least ir for indirect readers. And `protect` raises both levels by join with l.

The noninterference theorem with hypothesis about ir: if the secret's indirect level doesn't flow to the result's indirect level, then results are equal. This makes sense: the context may branch on the secret, causing implicit flows at level T_secret.ir; the result's ir must be at least that level for the flow to be allowed. If not, no dependence.

Or easier: define a function that eagerly constructs the relation as a *predicate transformer* using a fuel parameter:

```
Fixpoint Vf (n : nat) (l : label) (T : ty) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct n} : Prop :=
  match n with
  | 0 => True
  | S n' =>
      match T with
      | Ty_Unit _ => True
      | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ => visible l T -> (shape match with Vf n' l T1 ...)
      | Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ => visible -> (pair ... Vf n' l T1 ... Vf n' l T2 ...)
      | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ => visible -> (forall u1 u2 r, Vf n' l T1 u1 u2 -> flows κ.reader r -> Ef n' l (ty_protect κ.ir T2) (app ...))
      end
  end
with Ef (n : nat) (l : label) (T : ty) (t1 t2 : tm) {struct n} : Prop :=
  match n with
  | 0 => True
  | S n' => forall v1 v2, multi t1 v1 -> multi t2 v2 -> value v1 -> value v2 -> Vf n' l T v1 v2
  end.
```

Since ty_protect preserves size ≤ n', and we recurse with n' (strictly smaller fuel), guard check passes (structural on n). Then the final relation uses a sufficiently large fuel. But proofs get polluted by fuel: we'd need monotonicity in fuel and instantiate with e.g. `size T` or `S (size T)` everywhere, and the fundamental theorem by induction on typing doesn't obviously give enough fuel... We can set the fuel to a huge number (like `size T + size of term`?) and prove monotonicity: Vf n T -> Vf (S n) T? Actually Vf is monotone in fuel? At arrow case, more fuel means more recursive depth; likely Vf n implies Vf (S n) if the types' recursion depth ≤ n. Proving monotonicity requires nested induction. Messy but doable.

Actually maybe define the relation on types by recursion where the *type's own security is not part of the recursive structure*: i.e., define a relation parameterized by a "type skeleton" that excludes the root annotation, and pass the security separately. Something like:

```
Fixpoint V (l : label) (T : ty) : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => fun _ _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ => fun v1 v2 => flows κ.ir l -> shape...
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 κ => ...
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ => fun v1 v2 => flows κ.ir l -> forall u1 u2 r, V l T1 u1 u2 -> flows κ.reader r -> E_at_sec l (security_of T2) (κ.ir) T2? ...
```
where the codomain relation uses T2's skeleton but with the security modified to protect_security (security_of T2) κ.ir. If we pass the security as a separate parameter, then the recursion on the *type skeleton* is structural! Define a function `type_skel T` maybe, or define V on T with an extra security argument that overrides the root annotation:

```
Fixpoint V (l : label) (T : ty) (κ_loc : security) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 _ =>
      flows_to κ_loc.ir l ->
      (exists ..., V l T1 (security_of T1) ... )   (* children use their own security *)
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 _ => ...
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 _ =>
      flows_to κ_loc.ir l ->
      forall u1 u2 r, V l T1 (security_of T1) u1 u2 -> flows_to κ_loc.reader r ->
        E l T2 (protect_security (security_of T2) κ_loc.ir) (app ...)
  end
with E (l : label) (T : ty) (κ_loc : security) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2, multi t1 v1 -> multi t2 v2 -> value v1 -> value v2 -> V l T κ_loc v1 v2.
```

Hmm, E recursing with the same T: in the mutual fixpoint, E is defined with an extra parameter; but E's body calls V l T κ_loc (same T). Guard checker: mutual fixpoint with V recursing on T and E calling V on the same T — that's allowed? In a mutual fixpoint, recursive calls must be on structurally smaller arguments of the corresponding function. E calls V on T (not smaller) — violates guard? But V and E are in a mutual block; E's call to V with the same T might be rejected by guard checker ("recursive call on non-strictly smaller argument"). Actually Coq's guard checker requires each recursive call to be on a subterm of the corresponding function's argument; if E's argument is (l, T, κ_loc, t1, t2) and V's is (l, T, κ_loc, v1, v2), the call V l T κ_loc ... has the same T — not smaller. Rejected likely.

Unless E is not mutually recursive because V's arrow case can inline E's definition: define V's arrow case with the forall over evaluations directly, avoiding a separate E. Then V is a single Fixpoint; recursive calls in the arrow case: V l T1 κ1 ... uses T1 (subterm), and the forall-evaluation body calls V l T2 (protect_security (security_of T2) κ.ir) v1 v2 — T2 is a subterm, structural descent! Because the type argument is literally T2, with a different security parameter. That works! The only issue: E as a separate definition can then be defined *after* V as `E l T κ t1 t2 := forall v1 v2, multi ... -> V l T κ v1 v2`, and V's arrow case can inline this as a forall (since V is defined first). But wait, V's arrow case inlined would be:

```
| Ty_Arrow T1 T2 _ =>
    flows_to κ_loc.ir l ->
    forall u1 u2 r,
      V l T1 (security_of T1) u1 u2 ->
      flows_to κ_loc.reader r ->
      forall w1 w2,
        multi (tm_app v1 u1 r) w1 -> multi (tm_app v2 u2 r) w2 ->
        value w1 -> value w2 ->
        V l T2 (protect_security (security_of T2) κ_loc.ir) w1 w2
```

Here V's recursive call on T2 has an arbitrary security argument `protect_security (security_of T2) κ_loc.ir` — the type argument T2 is a direct subterm (from the match), so guard checker should accept since the recursive argument is T2 (structurally smaller). The extra security parameter can be anything (not recursive). Great! Similarly, the sum/prod cases recurse on T1,T2 with `security_of Ti` — direct subterms. Good.

But careful: `security_of T2` uses T2 as an argument to a function, in the security parameter, which isn't recursive. Fine.

So design:

```
Fixpoint V (l : label) (T : ty) (kappa : security) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 _ =>
      flows_to kappa.(indirect_reader) l ->
      ((exists v1' k1 v2' k2, v1 = tm_inl v1' k1 /\ v2 = tm_inl v2' k2 /\
          V l T1 (security_of T1) v1' v2')
       \/ (exists v1' k1 v2' k2, v1 = tm_inr v1' k1 /\ v2 = tm_inr v2' k2 /\
          V l T2 (security_of T2) v1' v2'))
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 _ =>
      flows_to kappa.(indirect_reader) l ->
      (exists a1 b1 k1 a2 b2 k2, v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 k2 /\
          V l T1 (security_of T1) a1 a2 /\ V l T2 (security_of T2) b1 b2)
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 _ =>
      flows_to kappa.(indirect_reader) l ->
      (forall u1 u2 r, V l T1 (security_of T1) u1 u2 ->
         flows_to kappa.(reader) r ->
         forall w1 w2,
           multi (tm_app v1 u1 r) w1 -> multi (tm_app v2 u2 r) w2 ->
           value w1 -> value w2 ->
           V l T2 (protect_security (security_of T2) kappa.(indirect_reader)) w1 w2)
  end.
```

Wait, in the arrow case, the recursive V calls on T2 have T2 as a direct subterm, but the security parameter `protect_security (security_of T2) κ.ir` mentions T2 — still fine for the guard since the recursive argument is the variable T2 (subterm of the match scrutinee). But there's a subtlety: Coq's guard condition requires the recursive call's structurally-decreasing argument to be a subterm of the original; T2 is. Good.

However, `V l T1 (security_of T1) u1 u2` in the *domain* of an implication: that's fine (Prop).

But wait, does V's recursion allow calling V at the same T in the arrow case? No, only T1,T2. Good, no same-type recursion. 

Now, `E` defined after V:

```
Definition E (l : label) (T : ty) (kappa : security) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop :=
  forall v1 v2, multi t1 v1 -> multi t2 v2 -> value v1 -> value v2 ->
    V l T kappa v1 v2.
```

Hmm, but why carry κ separately? For each type T, the security annotation in T may be overridden by κ. The "natural" use is E l T (security_of T). When we protect, E at ty_protect l T with security_of (ty_protect l T) = protect_security (security_of T) l. But we can also define E with an explicit security parameter to make rewriting easier. Actually maybe define E just as `E l T t1 t2 := forall v1 v2, ... V l T (security_of T) v1 v2`. The arrow case's result type ty_protect κ.ir T2 has security_of = protect_security (security_of T2) κ.ir, exactly matching the V call in the arrow case. Good: E l (ty_protect κ.ir T2) unfolds to V l (ty_protect κ.ir T2) (security_of (ty_protect κ.ir T2)) = V l (ty_protect κ.ir T2) (protect_security (security_of T2) κ.ir). But V's recursion matches on the type constructor and uses the passed κ parameter for the visibility/arrow-reader, ignoring the type's own stored security. So V l (ty_protect κ.ir T2) σ v1 v2 where σ = protect_security (security_of T2) κ.ir. But the arrow case's V call was V l T2 (protect_security (security_of T2) κ.ir) w1 w2. Since V ignores the stored security in T2 (the pattern `Ty_Arrow T1 T2 _`), V l (ty_protect κ.ir T2) σ w1 w2 = V l T2 σ w1 w2 definitionally? Let's check: `ty_protect κ.ir (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ2)` = Ty_Arrow T1 T2 (protect_security κ2 κ.ir), and V pattern-matches on T: sees Ty_Arrow T1 T2 _, ignores the third component, uses the explicit κ parameter σ. And V l T2 σ pattern matches on T2. These are the same unfolding if T2 has the same head. Yes! V l (ty_protect l0 T2) σ w1 w2 is definitionally equal to V l T2 σ w1 w2 for any l0, since ty_protect preserves the head constructor and children, and V ignores the stored security. That's a nice property! Let's verify for all constructors: ty_protect l0 (Ty_Unit κ) = Ty_Unit (protect κ l0), V ignores κ => True. Sum: Ty_Sum T1 T2 (protect κ l0), V ignores the security and uses explicit σ; children same T1,T2, recursive calls use security_of Ti (of the original Ti, same). So definitionally equal. Prod same. Arrow same. So `V l (ty_protect l0 T) σ v1 v2` is definitionally equal to `V l T σ v1 v2` for all T,l0,σ! Great, this avoids ty_protect rewriting headaches (at least for V; we may need the equality for the term relation E too, which unfolds to V).

Similarly, `V l T σ` ignores T's stored security entirely; it only uses the type structure (children) and σ. This is a clean design: the security annotation is a separate parameter. Then the relation on "type T at security σ" makes sense: T gives the structure, σ gives the level.

Now define the semantics of the relation and check the fundamental theorem at typing rules, with σ = security_of T where appropriate.

Key definitions:
```
Definition V (l : label) (T : ty) (κ : security) : tm -> tm -> Prop
Definition E (l : label) (T : ty) (κ : security) (t1 t2 : tm) :=
  forall v1 v2, multi t1 v1 -> multi t2 v2 -> value v1 -> value v2 -> V l T κ v1 v2.
```

For a type T, the natural relation is E l T (security_of T).

But why pass κ at all? Because when we protect a type, the security changes while structure stays. E.g., the arrow relation result type ty_protect κ.ir T2 with security protect_security (security_of T2) κ.ir; and V l (ty_protect κ.ir T2) that_security = V l T2 that_security. Good.

Let's now check the fundamental theorem with E at natural security. Statement:

Fundamental theorem: for all Γ, t, T, has_type Γ t T -> for all l, γ1, γ2, (forall x T', lookup_context x Γ = Some T' -> E l T' (security_of T') (γ1 (tm_fvar x)?) ...) -> E l T (security_of T) (γ1 t) (γ2 t).

We need a notion of closing substitution: functions from atoms to terms, and substitution applied to t. Since the language has fvar and subst, and typing uses "open body (tm_fvar x)" with freshness, the standard locally nameless proof uses a closing substitution and a lemma about open/subst composition.

Simpler approach given the typing rules with `forall x ∉ L, has_type (update Γ x T1) (open body (fvar x)) T2`: prove the fundamental theorem by induction on the typing derivation, using an environment of substitutions. Define `closing (γ : atom -> tm) (t : tm) := subst?` Hmm, our `subst x u t` substitutes a single free variable; we can define a simultaneous substitution or just use the environment as a function and prove a "subst composition" lemma. Standard locally nameless: define `subst_env γ t` recursively replacing tm_fvar x by γ x. But the T_Abs case needs to extend γ at x with a fresh variable and then use the "open body (fvar x)" typing; the closing of the abs term under γ: γ(tm_abs T body κ) = tm_abs T (close_body?) — if γ is simultaneous substitution, then (γ(abs T body κ)) = tm_abs T (subst_env γ body) κ, but body has bound variable 0 representing the argument; substituting free variables in body is fine, the bound vars remain tm_bvar. Then β-reduction opens the body with the argument value. We need a lemma relating subst_env and open: `subst_env γ (open body u) = open (subst_env γ body) (subst_env γ u)`? Hmm, with locally nameless: open body u substitutes u for bvar 0; subst_env substitutes fvars. They commute: subst_env γ (open body u) = open (subst_env γ body) (subst_env γ u). Actually, the cleanest: state and prove the "protect" lemma semantically:

Lemma protect_E : forall l T σ l0 t1 t2,
  E l T σ t1 t2 ->
  E l T (protect_security σ l0) (tm_protect l0 t1) (tm_protect l0 t2).

Proof: take v1 v2 with multi (tm_protect l0 t1) v1 etc. Need V l T (protect σ l0) v1 v2.

Let's define V with coherence:

```
V l (Ty_Unit _) σ v1 v2 := True
V l (Ty_Sum T1 T2 _) σ v1 v2 :=
  flows σ.ir l ->
  (exists k1 v1' k2 v2', v1 = tm_inl v1' k1 /\ v2 = tm_inl v2' k2 /\
     security_le k1 σ /\ security_le k2 σ /\ V l T1 (security_of T1) v1' v2')
  \/ (exists ..., inr ... /\ security_le k1 σ /\ security_le k2 σ /\ V ...)
V l (Ty_Prod T1 T2 _) σ v1 v2 :=
  flows σ.ir l ->
  exists a1 b1 k1 a2 b2 k2, v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 k2 /\
    security_le k1 σ /\ security_le k2 σ /\
    V l T1 (security_of T1) a1 a2 /\ V l T2 (security_of T2) b1 b2
V l (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 _) σ v1 v2 :=
  flows σ.ir l ->
  (exists T1' b1 k1, v1 = tm_abs T1' b1 k1 /\ security_le k1 σ) /\
  (exists T2' b2 k2, v2 = tm_abs T2' b2 k2 /\ security_le k2 σ) /\
  (forall u1 u2 r, V l T1 (security_of T1) u1 u2 -> flows σ.reader r ->
     forall w1 w2, multi (app v1 u1 r) w1 -> multi (app v2 u2 r) w2 ->
       value w1 -> value w2 -> V l T2 (protect_security (security_of T2) σ.ir) w1 w2)
```

In P (protection) arrow case, source V visible gives both abs; target values protect_value of abs are abs; coherence: protect_security k_i l0 ⊑ protect_security σ l0 given k_i ⊑ σ. Good — this actually simplifies P's proof: both are abs, no non-abs vacuity cases! Nice.

Now, what about V at sum/prod *coherence*: do we actually need coherence at pair/sum/arrow for the erase lemma? No. It's needed for the elimination rules' annotation mismatch. We include it in visible branches.

Let me now define V precisely:

```
Fixpoint V (l : label) (T : ty) (sigma : security) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ =>
      flows_to sigma.(indirect_reader) l ->
      (exists k1 k2, v1 = tm_unit k1 /\ v2 = tm_unit k2)
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 _ =>
      flows_to sigma.(indirect_reader) l ->
      ( (exists v1' k1 v2' k2,
           v1 = tm_inl v1' k1 /\ v2 = tm_inl v2' k2 /\
           security_le k1 sigma /\ security_le k2 sigma /\
           V l T1 (security_of T1) v1' v2')
        \/ (exists v1' k1 v2' k2,
           v1 = tm_inr v1' k1 /\ v2 = tm_inr v2' k2 /\
           security_le k1 sigma /\ security_le k2 sigma /\
           V l T2 (security_of T2) v1' v2') )
  | Ty_Prod T1 T2 _ =>
      flows_to sigma.(indirect_reader) l ->
      (exists a1 b1 k1 a2 b2 k2,
         v1 = tm_pair a1 b1 k1 /\ v2 = tm_pair a2 b2 k2 /\
         security_le k1 sigma /\ security_le k2 sigma /\
         V l T1 (security_of T1) a1 a2 /\
         V l T2 (security_of T2) b1 b2)
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 _ =>
      flows_to sigma.(indirect_reader) l ->
      (exists Ta1 b1 k1, v1 = tm_abs Ta1 b1 k1 /\ security_le k1 sigma) /\
      (exists Ta2 b2 k2, v2 = tm_abs Ta2 b2 k2 /\ security_le k2 sigma) /\
      (forall u1 u2 r,
         V l T1 (security_of T1) u1 u2 ->
         flows_to sigma.(reader) r ->
         forall w1 w2,
           multi (tm_app v1 u1 r) w1 ->
           multi (tm_app v2 u2 r) w2 ->
           value w1 -> value w2 ->
           V l T2 (protect_security (security_of T2) sigma.(indirect_reader)) w1 w2)
  end.
```

So revise V's arrow case:

```
| Ty_Arrow T1 T2 _ =>
    flows sigma.ir l ->
    (exists ..., v1 = tm_abs ... /\ security_le k1 sigma) /\
    (exists ..., v2 = tm_abs ... /\ security_le k2 sigma) /\
    (forall u1 u2 r,
       E_at l T1 (security_of T1) u1 u2 ->   (* inline: forall values, multi -> V *)
       flows_to sigma.reader r ->
       forall w1 w2,
         multi (tm_app v1 u1 r) w1 ->
         multi (tm_app v2 u2 r) w2 ->
         value w1 -> value w2 ->
         V l T2 (protect_security (security_of T2) sigma.ir) w1 w2)
```

But V is a Fixpoint on T; inlining E at T1 requires a forall over evaluations with V at T1 (subterm), which is fine structurally. Let's inline:

```
       (forall a1 a2, 
          (forall w1 w2, multi a1 w1 -> multi a2 w2 -> value w1 -> value w2 ->
             V l T1 (security_of T1) w1 w2) ->
          flows_to sigma.reader r ->
          forall w1 w2, multi (tm_app v1 a1 r) w1 -> multi (tm_app v2 a2 r) w2 ->
            value w1 -> value w2 ->
            V l T2 (protect_security (security_of T2) sigma.ir) w1 w2)
```

The recursive V calls at T1 and T2 are structural. Good. This is the standard "arrow relation with term-level arguments".

Now redefine E after V as:
```
Definition E (l T sigma t1 t2) :=
  forall v1 v2, multi t1 v1 -> multi t2 v2 -> value v1 -> value v2 -> V l T sigma v1 v2.
```
Then the arrow body is exactly E at T1 applied to a1,a2, and V at T2 after. We can't literally call E inside V, but we can inline as above; or use a `let`? Coq's Fixpoint body can use a local `Definition`? We can write the inlined form, and later prove a lemma that V arrow body is equivalent to the form with E (by unfolding E). Since E is defined after V, its unfolding is definitional; when we unfold V and E, the shapes match. Let's define V with the inlined E-form, and then prove a "smart constructor" lemma `V_arrow_unfold` or just rely on `simpl`/`unfold` to see them as equal. Since V's definition is a Fixpoint, `unfold V` might not fire nicely (Fixpoint needs `simpl` or `cbn`). We'll manage with lemmas.

Let me carefully define the factorization lemmas.

1. `multi_abs_inv : forall T b k w, multi (tm_abs T b k) w -> w = tm_abs T b k`. Actually multi_refl t gives multi t t; multi_step needs step t t2, impossible. Prove by induction on multi; in the step case, inversion on step gives a contradiction (abs as source). We can prove `abs_no_step : forall T b k t, ~ step (tm_abs T b k) t` by `intros; intro H; inversion H.` Similarly `unit_no_step`.

2. `multi_app_inv`:
```
Lemma multi_app_inv : forall f a r v,
  multi (tm_app f a r) v -> value v ->
  exists vf va w,
    multi f vf /\ value vf /\
    multi a va /\ value va /\
    (exists T b k, vf = tm_abs T b k) /\
    step (tm_app vf va r) w /\ multi w v.
```
Proof by induction on the multi derivation.
- multi_refl: v = app f a r and value v: contradiction (app is not a value). Need `app_not_value : forall f a r, ~ value (tm_app f a r)` by intro H; inversion H.
- multi_step: step (app f a r) t2, multi t2 v. Invert step:
  * ST_AppAbs T body kappa v0 r0: f = tm_abs T body kappa, a = v0, r = r0, t2 = tm_protect kappa.ir (open body v0), value (tm_abs...) and value v0. Take vf = f, va = a, w = t2. multi f f refl; value f from premise; multi a a refl; value a; abs shape; step (app vf va r) t2 — is that exactly ST_AppAbs with the same premises? ST_AppAbs requires value (tm_abs T body kappa) = value f, value v0 = value a, flows_to kappa.reader r; the flow premise is in the ST_AppAbs hypothesis from inversion. So constructor applies, giving step (app f a r) t2 which is exactly the given step. And multi t2 v given.
  * ST_App1 t1 t1' t2' r': f --> f1', t2 = tm_app f1' a r. Apply IH to multi t2 v: get vf va w with multi f1' vf, value vf, multi a va, value va, abs shape, step (app vf va r) w, multi w v. Then multi f vf via multi_step (f --> f1') (multi f1' vf). Done.
  * ST_App2 v1 t2 t2' r': value f, a --> a', t2 = tm_app f a' r. IH on multi t2 v gives vf va w with multi f vf, etc., multi a' va. Then multi a va via multi_step (a --> a'). Done.

Note: In the ST_AppAbs case, the inversion gives the hypothesis `value (tm_abs T body kappa)` and `value v` (named v0). Fine.

3. `multi_protect_inv`:
```
Lemma multi_protect_inv : forall l t v,
  multi (tm_protect l t) v -> value v ->
  exists w, multi t w /\ value w /\ v = protect_value w l.
```
Induction on multi:
- refl: v = tm_protect l t and value v: contradiction (`protect_not_value`? tm_protect is not a value: value constructors don't include protect. Prove by inversion).
- step: invert step:

We need multi t w where t = tm_protect l t0. We have step (tm_protect l t0) (tm_protect l t0') via ST_Protect, and multi (tm_protect l t0') w — wait IH gives multi t0' w, but we need multi (tm_protect l t0') w to apply multi_step at the protect level. Hmm, IH was applied to multi (tm_protect l t0') v, so it gives multi t0' w. That's the *inner* term relation, not the protect term. Let me re-examine: the inductive hypothesis is on multi t2 v where t2 = tm_protect l t0'. We need exists w, multi t w where t = tm_protect l t0. Take w from IH: multi t0' w (inner), but we need multi (tm_protect l t0) w. We have step (tm_protect l t0) (tm_protect l t0') and... we need multi (tm_protect l t0') w, but IH only gives multi t0' w and v = protect_value w l. Hmm. We know multi (tm_protect l t0') v and v = protect_value w l. But multi t0' w doesn't give multi (tm_protect l t0') w directly (protect of a multi converges to protect_value, which is w; but multi of the protect term to w needs the lemma we're proving!). Circular.

Let's strengthen the lemma or restructure. Actually the right statement: multi (tm_protect l t) v -> value v -> exists w, multi t w /\ value w /\ v = protect_value w l. We can prove it by induction on multi with a different strategy: in the ST_Protect case, we have t = tm_protect l t0. Use IH on multi (tm_protect l t0') v, which gives multi t0' w and v = protect_value w l. But we need multi (tm_protect l t0) w. We can derive: multi t0 t0' (one step), then multi (tm_protect l t0) (tm_protect l t0') by congruence of protect over multi, and then...? We need multi (tm_protect l t0') (protect_value w l). But that's exactly a special case: multi t0' w and value w -> multi (tm_protect l t0') (protect_value w l), which is the *other* lemma `multi_protect_result` (protected term evaluates to protected value). If we prove multi_protect_result first (by induction on multi, straightforward), then this works:
  multi (tm_protect l t0) (tm_protect l t0') (by congruence) then multi (tm_protect l t0') (protect_value w l) by multi_protect_result applied to multi t0' w. Then trans, v = protect_value w l. But wait, do we need multi_protect_result for the term tm_protect l t0' to value protect_value w l; yes: multi t0' w, value w ⟹ multi (tm_protect l t0') (protect_value w l). And v = protect_value w l, so trans. Good. So prove both lemmas, with multi_protect_inv depending on multi_protect_result, which is independent.

Actually, alternatively, derive multi_protect_inv from multi_protect_result? multi_protect_inv: multi (tm_protect l t) v -> exists w, multi t w /\ v = protect_value w l. Hmm, induction on multi with the ST_Protect case as above. Fine.

But wait: in the ST_Protect case, after IH we have multi t0' w, and by multi_protect_result multi (tm_protect l t0') (protect_value w l). And v = protect_value w l. So multi (tm_protect l t0) v via step + that. But the lemma wants multi t w with t = tm_protect l t0 — so take the same w: multi (tm_protect l t0) (protect_value w l) = multi t v; and v = protect_value w l. Wait, the lemma's conclusion is `exists w, multi t w /\ value w /\ v = protect_value w l` — with w the inner value; multi t w means multi (tm_protect l t0) w, and we have multi (tm_protect l t0) (protect_value w l) which equals v. So if v = protect_value w l, then multi t v = multi t (protect_value w l) which is what we have. Good.

Hmm, but note the lemma as stated gives multi t w where t is the protected term, which is trivial-ish: the result is v = protect_value w l. The point is to expose the underlying value w.

Simpler formulation: multi (tm_protect l t) v -> value v -> exists w, v = protect_value w l /\ multi t w /\ value w. Proof by induction on multi:
- refl: contradiction.
- ST_ProtectValue: t = v0 value, t2 = protect_value v0 l = v; take w = v0; multi v0 v0 refl.
- ST_Protect: t = tm_protect l t0, step t0 t0', t2 = tm_protect l t0', multi t2 v. IH: exists w, v = protect_value w l /\ multi t0' w /\ value w. Then multi (tm_protect l t0) w? We want multi t w with t = tm_protect l t0, i.e., multi (tm_protect l t0) w. We have multi_protect_result: multi t0' w -> multi (tm_protect l t0') (protect_value w l). Combined with step (tm_protect l t0) (tm_protect l t0'): multi (tm_protect l t0) (protect_value w l). And v = protect_value w l. So multi t v. Since v = protect_value w l, rewrite: multi t (protect_value w l). We need multi t w under the exists w...; we can take w and prove multi t w by rewriting v = protect_value w l into the goal: goal multi t w; we have multi t (protect_value w l); rewrite <- Hveq; exact. Good.

4. `multi_protect_result : forall l t v, multi t v -> value v -> multi (tm_protect l t) (protect_value v l)`. Induction on multi:
- refl: t = v value; ST_ProtectValue gives multi (protect v) (protect_value v l) via multi_step + multi_refl.
- step: step t t', multi t' v; IH gives multi (protect t') (protect_value v l); ST_Protect gives step (protect t) (protect t'); multi trans.

5. `multi_case_inv`: 
```
Lemma multi_case_inv : forall t b1 b2 r v,
  multi (tm_case t b1 b2 r) v -> value v ->
  exists vt, multi t vt /\ value vt /\
    ((exists u k, vt = tm_inl u k /\ value u /\ flows_to k.reader r /\
        exists w, step (tm_case vt b1 b2 r) w /\ multi w v)
     \/ (exists u k, vt = tm_inr u k /\ value u /\ flows_to k.reader r /\
        exists w, step (tm_case vt b1 b2 r) w /\ multi w v))
```
Hmm, this bundles the step. But if vt is inl but the flow condition fails, the case is stuck; but since the whole reaches a value, the step occurred. However, when proving this by induction, the ST_Case case (scrutinee steps) recurses and the final step's flow condition comes from the original step derivation. Let's structure as: `exists vt, multi t vt /\ value vt /\ exists w, step (tm_case vt b1 b2 r) w /\ multi w v` plus separately the shape of vt as inl/inr with flow premise. Actually the step (tm_case vt ...) w inversion gives the shape and flow. From `step (tm_case vt b1 b2 r) w` inversion: ST_CaseLeft (vt = inl u k, value u, flows k.reader r, w = tm_protect k.ir (open b1 u)); ST_CaseRight; ST_Case (vt steps — but value vt so impossible). So we can state:
```
exists vt w, multi t vt /\ value vt /\ step (tm_case vt b1 b2 r) w /\ multi w v
```
and then use step inversion later in the case proof. But in the proof of the case lemma by induction on multi: 
- refl: case term = v, value v — case not a value, contradiction.
- step: invert the step of the case term:
  * ST_CaseLeft v0 k body1 body2 r0: t = tm_inl v0 k, b1 = body1, b2 = body2, r = r0, t2 = tm_protect k.ir (open body1 v0), value v0, flows k.reader r. Take vt = tm_inl v0 k; multi t vt refl; value vt (v_inl with value v0); step (case vt b1 b2 r) t2 is the given step; multi t2 v. Done.
  * ST_CaseRight similar; take vt = inr.
  * ST_Case t0 t0' body1 body2 r0: t = tm_case t0 b1 b2 r, t0 --> t0', t2 = tm_case t0' b1 b2 r. Wait the step constructor is `ST_Case : forall t t' body1 body2 r, t --> t' -> tm_case t body1 body2 r --> tm_case t' body1 body2 r`. Inversion aligns: t0 steps to t0', and the resulting term is case t0' b1 b2 r. Apply IH to multi t2 v: get vt w with multi t0' vt, value vt, step (case vt b1 b2 r) w, multi w v. Then multi t0 vt via multi_step. Done.
So the lemma with `exists vt w, multi t vt /\ value vt /\ step (case vt b1 b2 r) w /\ multi w v` works. Could we simplify further to return the shape? In T_Case, after applying the lemma we get step (case vt ...) w; invert it to get the inl/inr shape and the stored annotation and flow. Then proceed.

But note: we also need the scrutinee value relation V from E on t1,t2; the lemma gives multi t_i vt_i and value vt_i. Good. Then E gives V l (Sum T1 T2 κ) κ vt1 vt2. Note: E's security parameter for the scrutinee is security_of (Ty_Sum T1 T2 κ) = κ. Good.

6. `multi_if_inv`: similar:

7. `multi_fst_inv` / `multi_snd_inv`:
```
exists vp w, multi t vp /\ value vp /\ step (tm_fst vp r) w /\ multi w v
```
Inversion: ST_FstPair, ST_Fst.

Now, in the T_Case proof, after `multi_case_inv` on both sides, we have `step (tm_case vt1 b1 b2 r) w1'` and `step (... vt2 ...) w2'`. Invert these steps. They must be ST_CaseLeft/ST_CaseRight. Then the scrutinee values vt_i = inl u_i k_i or inr. Note: the step relation for the left and right are independent; we need them to use the *same* branch. The V sum relation applied to vt1,vt2 tells us the shapes match (both inl or both inr) — but only in the visible case (flows κ.ir l). In the visible case, we can then choose the branch. The step inversion on the left gives maybe ST_CaseLeft; on the right, V sum forces vt2 = inl u2 k2, so its step must be ST_CaseLeft (ST_CaseRight impossible since vt2 = inl; ST_Case impossible since vt2 is a value). Good.

We need to align the step inversion results: e.g., Hstep1 : step (tm_case vt1 b1 b2 r) w1' and Hstep2. With vt1 = inl u1 k1 from inversion. Do the inversion after establishing the shapes; then `inversion Hstep1; subst` gives case ST_CaseLeft with b1, b2, etc., and w1' = tm_protect k1.ir (open b1 u1). Similarly right. But inversion on both may produce many cases; we need to kill ST_Case because vt1 is a value (no step). We have value vt1; ST_Case's premise would be vt1 --> _, contradicting value_no_step. So use a lemma `step_case_value_inv`:
```
Lemma step_case_value_inv : forall vt b1 b2 r w,
  value vt -> step (tm_case vt b1 b2 r) w ->
  (exists u k, vt = tm_inl u k /\ value u /\ flows_to k.reader r /\ w = tm_protect k.ir (open b1 u))
  \/ (exists u k, vt = tm_inr u k /\ value u /\ flows_to k.reader r /\ w = tm_protect k.ir (open b2 u)).
```
Prove by inversion; ST_Case case contradicts value_no_step.

Similarly `step_if_value_inv`, `step_app_value_inv` (with both f,a values), `step_fst_value_inv`.

For T_App, we use multi_app_inv which gives the step (app vf va r) w with value vf, value va. Then `step_app_value_inv` gives the abs shape: vf = tm_abs T b k, w = tm_protect k.ir (open b va). We already have abs shape from multi_app_inv; but the inversion gives w and flow directly. Maybe simplify multi_app_inv to return the step and then use step_app_value_inv. Let's restructure multi_app_inv to directly return the evaluated pieces and the resulting β-term:
```
Lemma multi_app_inv : forall f a r v,
  multi (tm_app f a r) v -> value v ->
  exists T body k va, multi f (tm_abs T body k) /\ value (tm_abs T body k) /\
    multi a va /\ value va /\ flows_to k.reader r /\
    multi (tm_protect k.ir (open body va)) v.
```
Let's check the induction proof:
- refl: app not value, contradiction.
- ST_AppAbs: f = tm_abs T body k, a = value v0, r; t2 = tm_protect k.ir (open body v0). Take T,body,k,va=v0. multi f f refl (abs = f); value abs from premise; multi a a refl; value v0; flows from ST_AppAbs premise; multi t2 v given.
- ST_App1: f --> f1', t2 = app f1' a r. IH gives T body k va, multi f1' (abs ...), ..., multi (tm_protect ...) v. Then multi f (abs) by multi_step. Done.
- ST_App2: value f, a --> a', t2 = app f a' r. IH gives T body k va, multi f (abs T body k), multi a' va, etc. Then multi a va by multi_step. Done.
This is cleaner. 

Similarly, multi_case_inv can directly return the branch term:
```
Lemma multi_case_inv : forall t b1 b2 r v,
  multi (tm_case t b1 b2 r) v -> value v ->
  exists vt,
    multi t vt /\ value vt /\
    ( (exists u k, vt = tm_inl u k /\ value u /\ flows_to k.reader r /\
         multi (tm_protect k.ir (open b1 u)) v)
      \/ (exists u k, vt = tm_inr u k /\ value u /\ flows_to k.reader r /\
         multi (tm_protect k.ir (open b2 u)) v) ).
```
Proof on multi:
- refl: case not value.
- step inversion:
  * ST_CaseLeft v0 k body1 body2 r0: t = inl v0 k, b1=body1, b2=body2, r=r0, t2 = tm_protect k.ir (open body1 v0), value v0, flows k.reader r. Take vt = inl v0 k: multi t vt refl; value; left disj with u=v0,k, multi branch term v = multi t2 v given.
  * ST_CaseRight similarly.
  * ST_Case: t0 step t0', t2 = case t0' b1 b2 r. IH on multi t2 v: exists vt, multi t0' vt, value vt, disj (with multi branch v). Then multi t0 vt by multi_step.
Good.

multi_if_inv:
```
Lemma multi_if_inv : forall c y n r v,
  multi (tm_if c y n r) v -> value v ->
  exists vc, multi c vc /\ value vc /\
    ((exists u k, vc = tm_inl u k /\ value u /\ flows_to k.reader r /\
        multi (tm_protect k.ir y) v)
     \/ (exists u k, vc = tm_inr u k /\ value u /\ flows_to k.reader r /\
        multi (tm_protect k.ir n) v)).
```
Similar.

multi_fst_inv:
```
forall t r v, multi (tm_fst t r) v -> value v ->
  exists vp, multi t vp /\ value vp /\
    exists v1 v2 k, vp = tm_pair v1 v2 k /\ value v1 /\ value v2 /\
      flows_to k.reader r /\ multi (tm_protect k.ir v1) v.
```
Proof on multi; refl contradiction (fst not value); ST_FstPair; ST_Fst recursion. Similarly snd.

Now, the fundamental theorem proof will use these factorizations plus E.

Let's also prove basic relation lemmas:

- `V_opaque : forall l T sigma v1 v2, ~ flows_to sigma.ir l -> V l T sigma v1 v2.` Proof: destruct T; simpl; intros; intro H; contradiction. Actually V is a Fixpoint; for each T constructor the definition is `flows sigma.ir l -> ...`, so proving it: destruct T; unfold V? `simpl` should reduce V once T is a constructor. Then `intros Hf; exfalso; apply H; exact Hf`. Hmm, V is a Fixpoint with struct T; `simpl V`? We can write `unfold V; fold V`? Let's test in Coq. Common approach: `destruct T; simpl; tauto` or `intros Hf; contradiction`. Let's see: after `destruct T`, the goal is `V l (Ty_Unit s) sigma v1 v2`; `simpl` should unfold V for a concrete constructor: gives `flows_to sigma.ir l -> exists..., ...`? Actually V's body for Ty_Unit is `flows ... -> True`? We'll define V unit as `flows ... -> exists k1 k2, ...`. So `simpl` gives `flows ... -> (exists ...)`. Then `intros Hcontra; exfalso; apply H; exact Hcontra`. For sum: `flows ... -> (A \/ B)`, same. For arrow: `flows ... -> (abs1 /\ abs2 /\ body)`. Same. Alternatively define our own max over a list with a simple Fixpoint and prove `n ∈ xs -> n ≤ max_list xs` and `max_list xs < S (max_list xs)`. That's easy and avoids library uncertainty.

```
Fixpoint max_list (xs : list nat) : nat :=
  match xs with
  | [] => 0
  | x :: xs' => Nat.max x (max_list xs')
  end.
Lemma in_max_list : forall xs x, In x xs -> x <= max_list xs.
Proof. induction xs; simpl; intros; [contradiction| destruct H; subst; [apply Nat.le_max_l | apply Nat.le_trans with (max_list xs); [apply IHxs; auto | apply Nat.le_max_r]]]. Qed.
Definition fresh (xs : list nat) := S (max_list xs).
Lemma fresh_notin : forall xs, ~ In (fresh xs) xs.
Proof. intros xs H; apply in_max_list in H; unfold fresh in H; apply Nat.nle_succ_diag_l? 
```

Now define `subst_env` and its properties:
```
Fixpoint subst_env (gamma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => gamma x
  | tm_unit k => tm_unit k
  | tm_abs T body k => tm_abs T (subst_env gamma body) k
  ...
  end.
```
(full recursion mirroring subst).

Lemmas:
- `subst_env_open_rec : forall gamma k u t, subst_env gamma (open_rec k u t) = open_rec k (subst_env gamma u) (subst_env gamma t)`. Prove by induction on t, generalizing k; unfold open_rec; simpl; rewrite IH; etc. Need to be careful with the bvar case: `Nat.eqb k i`; subst_env (bvar i) = bvar i; open_rec k u (bvar i) = if eqb k i then u else bvar i. LHS: subst_env (if eqb then u else bvar i) = if eqb then subst_env u else bvar i. RHS: open_rec k (subst_env u) (bvar i) = if eqb then subst_env u else bvar i. The if-distribution: destruct (Nat.eqb k i); reflexivity. Good.
- `subst_env_open : subst_env γ (open body u) = open (subst_env γ body) (subst_env γ u)` immediate from open_rec lemma with k=0.
- `subst_env_extend_absent`: for all γ x u t, ~ In x (fv t) -> subst_env (fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then u else γ y) t = subst_env γ t. From H : ~ In x [y], i.e., x ≠ y. Nat.eqb y x = false since y ≠ x. Use Nat.eqb_eq: eqb y x = true -> y = x. So destruct (Nat.eqb y x) eqn:H; [apply Nat.eqb_eq in H; contradiction | reflexivity]. Let's define a predicate:
```
Fixpoint all_ir_flows (l : label) (T : ty) : Prop :=
  flows_to (security_of T).(indirect_reader) l /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 _ | Ty_Prod T1 T2 _ | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 _ =>
      all_ir_flows l T1 /\ all_ir_flows l T2
  end.
```
Then prove: transparent T -> flows (security_of T).ir l -> all_ir_flows l T. Now, the final theorem's V_erase: we need `all_ir_flows` from transparent. Let's define:

```
Fixpoint ir_flows_to (l : label) (T : ty) : Prop :=
  flows_to (security_of T).(indirect_reader) l /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 _ | Ty_Prod T1 T2 _ | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 _ =>
      ir_flows_to l T1 /\ ir_flows_to l T2
  end.
```
Name it `all_ir_flows`.

`transparent_at_all_ir : forall l T sigma, flows_to sigma.ir l -> transparent_at sigma T -> all_ir_flows l T`. By induction on T:
- Unit: transparent_at sigma (Ty_Unit κ) = security_le (security_of (Ty_Unit κ)) sigma /\ True = security_le κ sigma /\ True. all_ir_flows l (Ty_Unit κ) = flows κ.ir l /\ True. From security_le κ sigma gives flows κ.ir sigma.ir; with flows sigma.ir l, trans.
- Sum/Prod/Arrow: transparent_at sigma T = security_le (security_of T) sigma /\ (transparent_at sigma T1 /\ transparent_at sigma T2). all_ir_flows l T = flows (security_of T).ir l /\ (all_ir_flows l T1 /\ all_ir_flows l T2). First: trans; children: IH with transparent_at sigma Ti and flows sigma.ir l. Good.

`transparent_all_ir : transparent T -> flows (security_of T).ir l -> all_ir_flows l T` by unfolding transparent as transparent_at (security_of T) T and applying the above with sigma = security_of T.

Now, the top-level `V_erase` uses all_ir_flows.

Now let's think about the induction structure in V_erase and V_sub, V_mono, V_protect, all of which are Fixpoints/Fixpoint-like recursions on ty. Since V is a Fixpoint, proofs by induction on T should work with `induction T`. Now the environment relation for Γ = [(x,T_secret)]: we need lookup_context y [(x,T_secret)] = Some T' -> E l T' (security_of T') (γ1 y)(γ2 y). Now, subst_env (plug C (fvar x)) commutation. Define `subst_env_plug : forall γ C t, subst_env γ (plug C t) = plug C (subst_env γ t)`. Prove by induction on C; all cases are structural and subst_env recurses. Should be straightforward with `simpl; rewrite IHC; ...`. Actually `plug` pattern matches C; subst_env of plug unfolds through the term constructors; e.g., C_Abs T C' κ: plug = tm_abs T (plug C' t) κ; subst_env = tm_abs T (subst_env γ (plug C' t)) κ = tm_abs T (plug C' (subst_env γ t)) κ = plug (C_Abs T C' κ) (subst_env γ t). Good. In some cases plug produces a term with two subterms where one is C' and another is a fixed term t0; subst_env γ will also substitute in t0. The RHS plug C (subst_env γ t) does NOT substitute into the fixed t0. So the equality fails unless γ substitutes fixed terms to themselves! Hmm! Right: plug C t contains embedded terms; subst_env γ of plug C t substitutes fvars in the embedded terms too; plug C (subst_env γ t) leaves embedded terms unchanged. So commutation requires γ to fix all fvars in C's embedded terms. Our γ_i maps y≠x to fvar y, so it fixes all fvars except x; and x ∉ fvs(C) by choice. Then subst_env γ_i (plug C (fvar x)) = plug C (subst_env γ_i (fvar x)) = plug C t_i. So we need a more refined lemma:

`subst_env_plug_agree : forall γ C t, (forall y, In y (fvs_context C) -> γ y = tm_fvar y) -> subst_env γ (plug C t) = plug C (subst_env γ t)`.

Or simpler: define γ_i as the identity except at x, and prove the commutation for such γ where C's fvars are all ≠ x. Let γ = fun y => if eqb y x then t_i else fvar y. Then subst_env γ s = s if x ∉ fv s, and = ... Let's prove a general lemma: if x ∉ fvs_context C, then subst_env γ (plug C u) = plug C (subst_env γ u) for γ = extend x t id. Prove by induction on C, using that for embedded terms t0 with x ∉ fv t0, subst_env γ t0 = t0. The latter is `subst_env_extend_absent` with γ = identity: subst_env γ t0 = subst_env id t0 = t0 (since x ∉ fv t0). So we need subst_env id t = t (easy) and subst_env_extend_absent.

Alternatively, prove a direct lemma `subst_env_plug_fresh : forall γ C u x, γ x = u -> (forall y, y <> x -> In y (fvs_context C) -> γ y = tm_fvar y) -> subst_env γ (plug C (tm_fvar x)) = plug C u`. This is more tailored. Hmm.

Maybe easier: avoid commutation with plug altogether by defining the environment on *the whole term* plug C (fvar x): we know subst_env γ (plug C (fvar x)) for γ = extend x u id equals plug C u if x is fresh from fvs(C). We can prove this by induction on C using a hypothesis `x ∉ fvs_context C` and facts about subst_env on embedded terms.

Let me define `fvs_context C` (list of all fvars in embedded terms; can include duplicates):
```
Fixpoint fvs_context (C : program_context) : list atom :=
  match C with
  | C_Hole => []
  | C_Abs _ C' _ => fvs_context C'
  | C_AppLeft C' t _ => fvs_context C' ++ fv t
  | C_AppRight t C' _ => fv t ++ fvs_context C'
  ...
  end
```
Then prove `fvs_plug : forall C t, fv (plug C t) = fvs_context C ++ fv t`? That's a clean way to know fvs(C) ⊆ fv(plug C t). Then choosing x ∉ fv (plug C (tm_fvar 0)) implies x ∉ fvs_context C. Actually fv (plug C (fvar 0)) = fvs_context C ++ [0]; so x ∉ it implies x ∉ fvs_context C (using In_app). Good, and we don't even need to state fvs_plug if we define fvs_context and prove the commutation lemma with hypothesis x ∉ fvs_context C.

Let's prove:
`lemma subst_env_plug : forall γ C t, (forall y, In y (fvs_context C) -> γ y = tm_fvar y) -> subst_env γ (plug C t) = plug C (subst_env γ t)`.
Induction on C; each case: unfold plug, subst_env (simpl), rewrite IHC (with hypothesis for C's subcontext), and for embedded terms t0, use the agreement hypothesis to show subst_env γ t0 = t0. The latter needs a lemma:
`subst_env_id_on : forall γ t, (forall y, In y (fv t) -> γ y = tm_fvar y) -> subst_env γ t = t`.
Prove by induction on t; fvar case uses hypothesis; composite cases use IH with membership in app. This is standard. Then the plug lemma uses this for embedded terms plus IHC for the context part. But there's a subtlety: in C_AppLeft C' t2 r, plug = tm_app (plug C' t) t2 r; subst_env = tm_app (subst_env (plug C' t)) (subst_env t2) r. RHS = tm_app (plug C' (subst_env t)) t2 r. Need subst_env t2 = t2 from hypothesis over fvs_context (C_AppLeft C' t2 r) = fvs_context C' ++ fv t2, so x ∈ fv t2 -> In in fvs_context; apply subst_env_id_on. Good. And IHC requires forall y ∈ fvs_context C', γ y = fvar y; follows from membership in the bigger list (In_app left). Manageable.

Alternatively define fvs_context to just be `fv (plug C (tm_fvar 0))` minus 0? Not a function of C structurally... fvs_context recursion is fine.

Then for our specific γ_i = extend x t_i id (i.e., fun y => if eqb y x then t_i else tm_fvar y):
- Hypothesis: forall y ∈ fvs_context C, γ_i y = fvar y. Since x ∉ fvs_context C (from x fresh), y ∈ fvs_context C -> y ≠ x -> eqb y x = false -> γ_i y = fvar y. Good.
- Then subst_env γ_i (plug C (fvar x)) = plug C (subst_env γ_i (fvar x)) = plug C (γ_i x) = plug C t_i (eqb x x true).
We also need `subst_env γ_i (fvar x) = γ_i x` by definition. Good.

Choose x fresh from `L ++ fvs_context C`? Since context_has_type's L and fvs_context C. Actually we can choose x = fresh (L ++ fvs_context C). Then x ∉ L and x ∉ fvs_context C, and x is not 0? fresh returns S(max) so ≥ 1, but no need. Great, no need for fv (plug...).

Alternative: prove multi_abs_inv directly by induction on Hm using `inversion Hm`? Actually there's a general scheme: use the fact that multi is the reflexive-transitive closure. Define a lemma:

`multi_step_inv : forall t1 t3, multi t1 t3 -> t1 = t3 \/ exists t2, step t1 t2 /\ multi t2 t3.`
Proof by induction on the multi derivation:
```
intros t1 t3 Hm. induction Hm.
- left; reflexivity.
- right. exists t2. split; [exact H | exact Hm].

So this lemma is proven without induction complexity:
```
Lemma multi_step_inv : forall t1 t3, multi t1 t3 -> t1 = t3 \/ exists t2, step t1 t2 /\ multi t2 t3.
Proof. intros t1 t3 Hm; destruct Hm; [left; reflexivity | right; eauto]. Qed.
```

Now V_erase and all_ir_flows/transparent_at_all_ir.

Define all_ir_flows:
```
Fixpoint all_ir_flows (l : label) (T : ty) : Prop :=
  flows_to (security_of T).(indirect_reader) l /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Unit _ => True
  | Ty_Sum T1 T2 _ | Ty_Prod T1 T2 _ | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 _ =>
      all_ir_flows l T1 /\ all_ir_flows l T2
  end.
```
Note the match's three constructors with identical body: Coq syntax allows multiple patterns with same body as in the file.

Lemma transparent_all_ir : forall (l : label) (T : ty),

Excellent! V_erase compiles. Now the biggest part: the substitution infrastructure and the fundamental theorem.

Let me now write:
1. subst_env definition.
2. subst_env_open_rec.
3. subst_env_extend_absent (or a general agreement lemma).
4. fvs_context and subst_env_plug.
5. Fundamental theorem.

First, subst_env:
```
Fixpoint subst_env (gamma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => gamma x
  | tm_unit k => tm_unit k
  | tm_abs T body k => tm_abs T (subst_env gamma body) k
  | tm_app t1 t2 r => tm_app (subst_env gamma t1) (subst_env gamma t2) r
  | tm_pair t1 t2 k => tm_pair (subst_env gamma t1) (subst_env gamma t2) k
  | tm_fst t1 r => tm_fst (subst_env gamma t1) r
  | tm_snd t1 r => tm_snd (subst_env gamma t1) r
  | tm_inl t1 k => tm_inl (subst_env gamma t1) k
  | tm_inr t1 k => tm_inr (subst_env gamma t1) k
  | tm_case t0 b1 b2 r => tm_case (subst_env gamma t0) (subst_env gamma b1) (subst_env gamma b2) r
  | tm_protect l t1 => tm_protect l (subst_env gamma t1)
  | tm_if c y n r => tm_if (subst_env gamma c) (subst_env gamma y) (subst_env gamma n) r
  end.
```

subst_env_open_rec:
```
Lemma subst_env_open_rec : forall gamma k u t,
  subst_env gamma (open_rec k u t) = open_rec k (subst_env gamma u) (subst_env gamma t).
Proof.
  intros gamma k u t. revert k. induction t; intros k; simpl; 
  try reflexivity;
  try (destruct (Nat.eqb k n) eqn:E; simpl; reflexivity).
```

- fvar x: open_rec = fvar x; subst_env = gamma x; RHS open_rec ... (gamma x) = gamma x (bvar? no, gamma x is a term not bvar 0; open_rec only substitutes bvar k, so open_rec k (...) If gamma x contains tm_bvar k, open_rec would replace it with subst_env u. Hmm, but the LHS subst_env (fvar x) = gamma x, not opened. So the equality FAILS if gamma x contains bvar k!

Alternative: formulate the substitution/open interaction differently to avoid opening γ images. We want subst_env γ' (open body (fvar x)) = open (subst_env γ body) u. We can prove this *specifically* using the fact that x is not in fv body (chosen fresh) and the environment γ' = γ ∪ {x ↦ u}. Let's prove a specialized lemma:

`subst_env_open_fresh : forall γ x u body, ~ In x (fv body) -> subst_env (extend x u γ) (open body (fvar x)) = open (subst_env γ body) u.`

And subst_env (extend x u γ) (open_rec 0 (fvar x) body): the occurrences of fvar x in open body (fvar x) come either from the opening (bvar 0 in body) or from body's own fvars; but x ∉ fv body, so all fvar x occurrences come from the opening. Hence subst_env maps them to u, and we get open (subst_env γ body) u. The subtle case of bvar k in γ images doesn't arise because we're not opening γ images: we open body's bvar 0 with fvar x (a variable), then substitute the environment which maps x to u and other vars to γ. The environment may map other variables to terms with bvars; those terms are substituted into fvar positions, not opened. So the lemma holds without local-closure assumptions!

Let's state:
```
Lemma subst_env_open_fresh : forall (gamma : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (u : tm) (body : tm),
  ~ In x (fv body) ->
  subst_env (fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then u else gamma y)
            (open body (tm_fvar x))
  = open (subst_env gamma body) u.
```

Fixpoint max_list (xs : list nat) : nat :=
  match xs with [] => 0 | x :: xs' => Nat.max x (max_list xs') end.
Lemma in_max_list : forall (xs : list nat) (x : nat), In x xs -> x <= max_list xs.
Proof. induction xs; simpl; intros; [contradiction|]. destruct H as [-> | H].
  - apply Nat.le_max_l.
  - eapply Nat.le_trans; [apply IHxs; exact H | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.
Definition fresh (xs : list atom) : atom := S (max_list xs).
Lemma fresh_notin : forall xs, ~ In (fresh xs) xs.
Proof. intros xs H; apply in_max_list in H; unfold fresh in H; lia. Qed.
```

Substitution infrastructure compiles. Now the fvs_context and subst_env_plug lemma, then the fundamental theorem.

fvs_context:
```
Fixpoint fvs_context (C : program_context) : list atom :=
  match C with
  | C_Hole => []
  | C_Abs _ C' _ => fvs_context C'
  | C_AppLeft C' t _ => fvs_context C' ++ fv t
  | C_AppRight t C' _ => fv t ++ fvs_context C'
  | C_PairLeft C' t _ => fvs_context C' ++ fv t
  | C_PairRight t C' _ => fv t ++ fvs_context C'
  | C_Fst C' _ => fvs_context C'
  | C_Snd C' _ => fvs_context C'
  | C_Inl C' _ => fvs_context C'
  | C_Inr C' _ => fvs_context C'
  | C_CaseScrutinee C' t1 t2 _ => fvs_context C' ++ fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | C_CaseLeft t0 C' t2 _ => fv t0 ++ fvs_context C' ++ fv t2
  | C_CaseRight t0 t1 C' _ => fv t0 ++ fv t1 ++ fvs_context C'
  | C_Protect _ C' => fvs_context C'
  | C_IfCondition C' y n _ => fvs_context C' ++ fv y ++ fv n
  | C_IfThen c C' n _ => fv c ++ fvs_context C' ++ fv n
  | C_IfElse c y C' _ => fv c ++ fv y ++ fvs_context C'
  end.
```
Note list app is right-associative: `a ++ b ++ c` = a ++ (b ++ c). Membership reasoning must match the order. Actually I can define fvs_context in a form where the hole's context fvs come first and use In_app; the exact associativity affects the proofs.

subst_env_plug:
```
Lemma subst_env_plug : forall (gamma : atom -> tm) (C : program_context) (t : tm),
  (forall x, In x (fvs_context C) -> gamma x = tm_fvar x) ->
  subst_env gamma (plug C t) = plug C (subst_env gamma t).
Proof.
  intros gamma C. induction C; intros t Hagree; simpl.
```
Each case: unfold plug; simpl subst_env; unfold fvs_context in Hagree? Let's handle.

For each C, plug produces a term with the recursive plug C' t and possibly embedded terms. subst_env of that term substitutes into both. RHS plug C (subst_env gamma t) has embedded terms unchanged. So we need subst_env gamma embedded = embedded, which follows from Hagree + subst_env_id_on (a lemma: subst_env gamma s = s if gamma agrees with fvar on fv s).

Let me prove `subst_env_id_on : forall gamma t, (forall x, In x (fv t) -> gamma x = tm_fvar x) -> subst_env gamma t = t`. Proof: rewrite <- subst_env_id t; apply subst_env_agree; intros x Hx; rewrite Hagree; reflexivity... Actually subst_env_id : subst_env (fun x => fvar x) t = t. So subst_env_agree gamma id t Hagree gives subst_env gamma t = subst_env id t = t.

For the plug lemma, in each case:
- C_Hole: plug = t; subst_env gamma t = plug C (subst_env gamma t) trivially.
- C_Abs T C' κ: plug = tm_abs T (plug C' t) κ. subst_env = tm_abs T (subst_env gamma (plug C' t)) κ. rewrite IHC' with the agreement restricted: forall x, In x (fvs_context C') -> gamma x = fvar x; follows from Hagree since fvs_context (C_Abs ...) = fvs_context C'. Then goal tm_abs T (plug C' (subst_env gamma t)) κ = plug (C_Abs T C' κ)(subst_env gamma t) = tm_abs T (plug C' ...) κ. Reflexivity.
- C_AppLeft C' t2 r: plug = tm_app (plug C' t) t2 r. subst_env = tm_app (subst_env (plug C' t)) (subst_env t2) r. RHS = tm_app (plug C' (subst_env t)) t2 r. Rewrite IHC' and subst_env_id_on t2 (agreement: x ∈ fv t2 -> x ∈ fvs_context (C_AppLeft C' t2 r) = fvs_context C' ++ fv t2 -> apply in_or_app right). Order: fvs_context C' ++ fv t2, so fv t2 is the right part.
- C_AppRight t1 C' r: fvs = fv t1 ++ fvs_context C'. Agreement for C' from right; for t1 from left.
- Pair similar.
- Fst/Snd/Inl/Inr: only C'.
- CaseScrutinee: fvs = fvs_context C' ++ fv t1 ++ fv t2 = fvs_context C' ++ (fv t1 ++ fv t2). So t1 membership: right, left; t2: right, right.
- CaseLeft: fv t0 ++ fvs_context C' ++ fv t2 = fv t0 ++ (fvs_context C' ++ fv t2). For C': right, left; t0: left; t2: right,right.
- CaseRight: fv t0 ++ fv t1 ++ fvs_context C' = fv t0 ++ (fv t1 ++ fvs_context C'). t0 left; t1 right,left; C' right,right.
- Protect: C'.
- IfCondition: fvs C' ++ fv y ++ fv n; y right,left; n right,right.
- IfThen: fv c ++ fvs C' ++ fv n; C' right,left; c left; n right,right.
- IfElse: fv c ++ fv y ++ fvs C'; c left; y right,left; C' right,right.

To avoid repeating, write the proof with `simpl; rewrite IHC'; ...` and helper `subst_env_id_on` plus `rewrite` with agreement lemmas.

subst_env_plug compiles! Now the fundamental theorem. This is the core. Let me write it carefully.

```
Theorem fundamental : forall (Gamma : context) (t : tm) (T : ty),
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall (l : label) (gamma1 gamma2 : atom -> tm),
    (forall x, locally_closed (gamma1 x)) ->
    (forall x, locally_closed (gamma2 x)) ->
    (forall (x : atom) (T' : ty), lookup_context x Gamma = Some T' ->
       E l T' (security_of T') (gamma1 x) (gamma2 x)) ->
    E l T (security_of T) (subst_env gamma1 t) (subst_env gamma2 t).
```

Proof by induction on the typing derivation. Let me write with explicit `as` patterns again, since the environments l,gamma1,gamma2 must be universally quantified in the IHs. The statement puts them after H; `induction H` with the goal being a forall — the IHs will be generalized? To be safe, use:
```
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T H.
  induction H; intros l gamma1 gamma2 Hlc1 Hlc2 Henv.
```

I need E_sub:
```
Lemma E_sub : forall (T U : ty), subtype T U ->
  forall l sigma sigma' t1 t2, security_le sigma sigma' ->
    E l T sigma t1 t2 -> E l U sigma' t1 t2.
Proof.
  intros T U Hsub. induction Hsub; intros l sigma sigma' t1 t2 Hle HE; unfold E in *; intros v1 v2 Hm1 Hm2 Hv1 Hv2; apply ... 
```
Actually simpler: E l T σ t1 t2 ==> forall v1 v2, multis -> V l T σ; then V_sub gives V l U σ'. So:
```
Lemma E_sub : forall T U, subtype T U -> forall l sigma sigma' t1 t2,
  security_le sigma sigma' -> E l T sigma t1 t2 -> E l U sigma' t1 t2.
Proof.
  intros T U Hsub l sigma sigma' t1 t2 Hle HE.
  unfold E in *. intros v1 v2 Hm1 Hm2 Hv1 Hv2.
  apply (V_sub T U Hsub l sigma sigma' v1 v2 Hle).
  apply HE; assumption.
Qed.
```
where V_sub is the lemma proven.

I need a helper for lookup/update:
```
Lemma lookup_update_same : forall Gamma x T, lookup_context x (update Gamma x T) = Some T.
Lemma lookup_update_other : forall Gamma x y T, x <> y -> lookup_context y (update Gamma x T) = lookup_context y Gamma.
```
Proof: unfold update, lookup_context; simpl; destruct (Nat.eqb x y)... Wait lookup_context y ((x,T)::Γ) = if eqb y x then Some T else lookup_context y Γ. For same y=x: eqb x x = true. For other: eqb y x = false from y ≠ x.

```
Lemma lookup_update_same : forall Gamma x T,
  lookup_context x (update Gamma x T) = Some T.
Proof. intros; unfold update, lookup_context. simpl. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity. Qed.
```
Careful: lookup_context matches on the list; ((x,T)::Γ) matches the second branch: `if Nat.eqb x x then Some T else lookup_context x Γ`; then rewrite Nat.eqb_refl.

```
Lemma lookup_update_other : forall Gamma x y T, y <> x ->
  lookup_context y (update Gamma x T) = lookup_context y Gamma.
Proof. intros; unfold update, lookup_context. simpl. destruct (Nat.eqb y x) eqn:E.
  - apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; contradiction.
  - reflexivity. Qed.
```
Also extend_env at x and at y≠x:
```
Lemma extend_env_same : forall γ x u, extend_env γ x u x = u.
Proof. intros; unfold extend_env; rewrite Nat.eqb_refl; reflexivity.
Lemma extend_env_other : forall γ x y u, y <> x -> extend_env γ x u y = γ y.
Proof. intros; unfold extend_env; destruct (Nat.eqb y x) eqn:E; [apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; contradiction | reflexivity].
```

Alternative: Use the induction principle directly by `apply has_type_ind` with a custom motive! Define the motive and pass explicit arguments. This gives full control:

```
Definition fundamental_motive (Gamma : context) (t : tm) (T : ty) : Prop :=
  forall l gamma1 gamma2,
    (forall x, locally_closed (gamma1 x)) ->
    (forall x, locally_closed (gamma2 x)) ->
    (forall x T', lookup_context x Gamma = Some T' -> E l T' (security_of T') (gamma1 x)(gamma2 x)) ->
    E l T (security_of T) (subst_env gamma1 t) (subst_env gamma2 t).

Theorem fundamental : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> fundamental_motive Gamma t T.
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T H. apply has_type_ind with (P := fundamental_motive); ...
```
The induction principle has_type_ind : forall P : context -> tm -> ty -> Prop, (forall Gamma x T, lookup... -> wf -> P Gamma (fvar x) T) -> ... -> forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> P Gamma t T. We need to provide each case. This is verbose but gives exact control. The constructors' arguments are named by us in each `intros`. Let's do this! It's like writing the induction principle manually.

The cases:
1. `forall (Gamma : context) (x : atom) (T : ty), lookup_context x Gamma = Some T -> wf_ty T -> P Gamma (tm_fvar x) T`.
   intros Gamma x T Hlook Hwf l γ1 γ2 Hlc1 Hlc2 Henv. unfold P? The goal is E l T (security_of T) (subst_env γ1 (fvar x)) (subst_env γ2 (fvar x)). simpl. apply Henv; exact Hlook.
2. T_Unit: intros Gamma kappa Hwf l γ1 γ2 ...; simpl; unfold E; ...
3. T_Abs: intros L Gamma T1 body T2 kappa HwfT1 Hwfκ IHbody l γ1 γ2 Hlc1 Hlc2 Henv.
   Here IHbody : forall x, ~ In x L -> P (update Gamma x T1) (open body (fvar x)) T2. When we need it for a chosen x, `pose proof (IHbody x Hx) as IHx`; then IHx is the motive, which we can apply to l γ1' γ2' Hlc... .
4. T_App: intros Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2 kappa r IH1 IH2 Hflow l γ1 γ2 Hlc1 Hlc2 Henv. Where IH1 : P Gamma t1 (Ty_Arrow T1 T2 κ), IH2 : P Gamma t2 T1.
5. etc.

This is definitely clearer. The motive P defined as a Definition, and the goals after `apply has_type_ind` with P := fundamental_motive will unfold to the expected forms. Let me write it this way.

I'll define:
```
Definition P (Gamma : context) (t : tm) (T : ty) : Prop :=
  forall (l : label) (gamma1 gamma2 : atom -> tm),
